### CONVERSATIONS.

an ill-advised man. He had red and fierce eyes, a pale face, a short black coat, a woolen belt over his cassock <note>The original French uses "soutanelle," a small cassock or clerical garment.</note>, a peculiar beard, and the air and hair of an offended devotee. "Sir," I said to him quite civilly, while gently seizing the hand holding his staff, "before I respond to the polite compliment you have come so far to bring me, have the goodness to tell me if you are a <term>Cabalist: A follower of Kabbalah, often associated in this era with mystical or occult knowledge.</term>, a <term>Rabbi: A Jewish scholar or teacher.</term>, or a <term>Rosicrucian: A member of a secret brotherhood claiming to possess ancient esoteric wisdom.</term>."

"I am Master Jean le Brun," he replied to me, "the great *Jordanus Brunus* <note>original: "Jordanus Brunus" — the Latin name for Giordano Bruno (1548–1600), the Italian philosopher and occultist who was burned at the stake for heresy.</note> was my great-great-grandfather, and you are an ill-advised man and an ignoramus."

"Master Jean le Brun," I countered, "I agree with the second honor; for I know neither you nor your great-great-grandfather: but teach me what reason you have to give me the first epithet, and to come from the depths of Ireland to me..."

[satisfac]tion that you could desire. "I want none of it, Mr. Jean le Brun," I said to him; "simply repair a small harm with a very great good: share with me your knowledge and your friendship."

"I give them to you," he said to me, placing his hand in mine. "You even seem to me a person well-suited for the great designs I have formed since my youth. Your character <note>original: 'morale'. In this context, it refers to the narrator’s personal ethics or temperament.</note> is good, since you suffer insults without being moved, and your Philosophy may yet become so, since you are not infatuated with *Aristotle*. Ah! *Aristotle*, what harm you have done to good morals, and how those Councils <note>Le Brun likely refers to the provincial councils, such as those in Paris in 1210 and 1215, which initially prohibited the teaching of Aristotle's works on natural philosophy.</note> which formerly forbade the reading of your works were well-inspired by Heaven! Are you not of this opinion, Sir? Is *Aristotle* not pernicious to Morality?"

"Pernicious," I replied, "Sir, pernicious to the last degree <note>original: 'du dernier pernicieux'. An idiomatic expression meaning 'extremely' or 'utterly' harmful.</note>." And

# CONVERSATIONS.

"Do you not find the century," <note>original: 'le siècle'. In this context, it refers to 'the age' or the current state of the world.</note> he continued, "horribly corrupt?"

"Horribly," I replied.

"And would you not willingly become," he went on, "the Disciple and the Coadjutor <note>Coadjutor: A formal term for an assistant, often a high-ranking ecclesiastical helper with the right of succession.</note> of a man extraordinarily raised up by the Holy Spirit for the general reformation of morals?"

"Provided that it were neither Luther nor Calvin," I replied, "nor some other man of the same kind, I have quite a penchant for correcting."

"That is," he said, "the most laudable inclination a Christian can have. One must think only of morals; we are all Pastors <note>Pastors: Used here in the sense of 'shepherds,' implying a mutual responsibility for spiritual guidance.</note> to one another; God has charged each of us in particular with the salvation of our neighbor. Woe to him who works only toward his own sanctification <note>sanctification: The process of being made holy or purified from sin.</note> and who neglects that of his brothers. But it is doing nothing to correct individual faults, or to oppose them only in

"Ah! Monsieur," I said to him, "let me share in this glory; make known to me this evil, and allow me to help you cure it."

All these **Visionaries** <note>original: 'Visionnaires' — in this context, it refers to those who create grand, often impractical intellectual or spiritual systems</note> who set themselves up as **Reformers**, and who spend their lives meditating on new **Laws**, a new **Politics**, a new **Theology**, a new **Morality**, and a new **Philosophy**, always possess something of the good and something of the ridiculous. They have certain lucid intervals where there is something to be gained; one laughs at the rest, and admires how far the imagination of a **Man of Letters** can be exalted. "Monsieur," said I to *Joannes Brunus* <note>Latin: Giordano Bruno (1548–1600), the Italian philosopher and friar famous for his theories on the infinite universe; he was executed by the Inquisition</note>, "you shall not leave, if you please; you are fatigued by your long journey, and you shall rest here. Here is a small parlor bed where you may lie down for a time; and regarding the consultation you wish—"

### *SECOND CONVERSATION.*

**Giordano Bruno** <note>original: 'Joannes Brunus' — the Latinized name of the Italian friar and philosopher known for his theories on the infinite universe</note> spent an hour in conference with the **Holy Spirit**: he emerged from my **study** <note>original: 'Cabinet' — a private room for work and reflection</note> inflamed like a **Cherub** <term>Cherub: in 17th-century theology, these were angels of the second highest order, often associated with divine light and zeal</term>. "You are one of us, my son," he told me, "God has told me that the zeal you possess for the **reformation of morals** <note>original: 'réformation des mœurs' — a typical Enlightenment-era goal of improving public ethics and religious behavior</note> comes from Him; that it is He who inspired in you the contempt you hold for **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle's traditional philosophy was often the target of "modern" thinkers of this era</note>; and that it is He who led you to understand that the melancholic **Descartes** <note>René Descartes: the French philosopher; Bruno's character here dismisses his rationalist approach in favor of his own mystical insights</note> does not deserve all the esteem that the author of this book would have one grant him. Upon these three foundations, I shall have no difficulty in sharing my designs with you, telling you my history, explaining my **Philosophy**, and inviting you to share in the glory of reforming the **Christian World**. Sit down, then,"

<summary>After an hour of private prayer, Giordano Bruno claims to have received divine approval to share his secret philosophy and plans for global moral reform with the narrator, based on their shared dislike of Aristotle and Descartes.</summary>

<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Holy Spirit, Aristotle, René Descartes, moral reform, Christian world, philosophy, 17th-century dialogue</keywords>

So then, Monsieur, I replied to him; I
am going to listen to you with all the docili-
ty of which I am capable. He sat down &
spoke in this manner.

"These recent times have been fertile in
**Reformers**. Hell seems to have
opened all its gates to overturn the **Barque of Peter** <note>original: 'Nacelle de Pierre' — a traditional metaphor for the Catholic Church, representing the boat from which Saint Peter fished</note>, under the pretext of
repairing it... God, always faithful to
the promise He made to her, that the
gates of Hell shall never prevail
against her, has also raised up on His
side extraordinary **Men**
to save her by the same means
through which the **Emissaries of Hell** have
tried to destroy her. A true zeal for
a **general Reformation** <term>General Reformation: a movement aimed at a total overhaul of both religious and social life to return to perceived original spiritual purity</term> has animated
several great Personages to
work with tireless care to
restore the purity of **primitive Morality** <note>original: 'Morale primitive' — the ethical and spiritual standards of the earliest Christians, often contrasted with the perceived corruption of the modern Church</note>: but by a secret judgment of
God, their holy efforts have been useless—"

<summary>Giordano Bruno begins his discourse by framing the religious conflicts of the era as a battle between demonic "Emissaries of Hell" and divinely inspired reformers seeking to restore the Church to its primitive purity.</summary>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>NEW</header>

them. I once conferred with most of these great Men; I told them my sentiments, but they did not wish to believe me: I am not surprised if they were unable to succeed. One of them wanted to first undertake the restoration of the ancient vigor of Discipline <term>Discipline: the system of rules and practices governing the conduct of clergy and laity within the Church</term>, & the severity of the old Canons <term>Canons: the laws or decrees established by a church council</term>. His design failed: one should not have gone so openly against the torrent of the century's corruption; the human heart requires more careful handling. Another, in league with the first, undertook a prodigious study to change the face of all Theology, to discredit the Scholastic Doctors <note>original: 'Docteurs Scholaſtiques' — medieval theologians like Thomas Aquinas who used logic and dialectic to explain Christian doctrine</note>, & to substitute for their reasoning a science of memory and collections based on the Church Fathers <note>original: 'Peres' — the early influential Christian theologians whose works were often contrasted with later Scholastic logic</note>. This design was grand and good: but, good God, what an undertaking! To openly defy <note>original: 'rompre en viſiere' — literally "to break across the visor," a jousting term meaning to confront someone directly and aggressively</note> the Pedants, the Universities, & the Monks! May God gran—

<summary>Giordano Bruno explains that previous reformers failed because they were too confrontational: one tried to enforce strict ancient laws against a corrupt age, while another tried to dismantle the entire Scholastic system of the universities.</summary>

I first asked him, following this method, to suppose that everything he had heard, and everything he had believed to be true until then..."

The Manichaean smiled. "Mr. Doctor," he said to me, "I ask you, if you please, what does *I doubt* mean? For I have forgotten. Could it by chance be the same thing as *I am in doubt*?"

"That is the very thing," I told him.

"That is to say," he continued, "that you reason so learnedly and artlessly that you are, because you are: *I am in doubt, therefore I am* is a charming demonstration; and as long as you say, *I am, therefore I am*, no one will be able to dispute that the conclusion is...

The Manichaean seemed little moved by all of this. "Before responding to your so convoluted demonstration," he told me, "we would first have to agree on many things, upon which I fear you have hardly meditated."

### THIRD CONVERSATION.

Mr. Jean le Brun ate dinner without saying a word: I noticed that he was extraordinarily thirsty. After the meal, he offered a lengthy prayer of thanks, then, approaching the fire, he cried out: "If we had Faith as small as a grain of mustard seed <note>A reference to Matthew 17:20, where Jesus suggests that even tiny amounts of faith can move mountains.</note>, we would have no need to eat or to drink so much; for it is written, that the just man lives by Faith and by the word of God <note>Jean le Brun is blending several scriptural passages here, including Habakkuk 2:4 and Matthew 4:4.</note>; Faith nourished Elijah and Moses for forty days <note>Refers to the miraculous forty-day fasts of these biblical prophets while in the presence of God.</note>."

"I believe, Monsieur," I said to him, "that when the Son of Man <note>A messianic title used in the New Testament; the narrator is quoting Luke 18:8, which questions if faith will exist at the end of time.</note> returns, he will hardly find any of this nourishing Faith on the earth. ### FOURTH CONVERSATION.

A short time later, Monsieur Jean le Brun woke up. "Praise be to God, my son," he said to me as he passed into my room. "Blessed be God, who watches over the salvation of His servants while they sleep, and who comes to illuminate the <term>vapors of sleep|In early modern medicine and philosophy, 'vapors' were thought to be physical exhalations rising from the stomach to the brain, which influenced the nature of dreams and sleep.</term> with the lights of His grace."

"Does God also speak to you while you sleep?" I asked him.

"Sometimes," he replied; "but for today He did not speak to me in person; He only sent me an **Angel of Peace** to announce His will to me, and to command me to reconcile with Mr. **Descartes**.

"With **Descartes**!" I exclaimed, "Mr. Jean le Brun! This so-called Angel is a **spirit of darkness**, transfigured into an **Angel of Light**." <note>Original: "Ange de lumiere." This is a biblical allusion to 2 Corinthians 11:14, where Paul warns that "Satan himself masquerades as an angel of light." The narrator is suggesting that le Brun's pro-Cartesian vision is a diabolical trick.</note>

"Not at all," he replied: "Learn,"

…am very satisfied with him. It is certain, as he told me very well, that when one writes a book, one must accommodate **weak minds** <note>original: *esprits foibles*; refers to those who rely on simple faith and traditional authority rather than independent reasoning</note> as much as satisfy **strong minds** <note>original: *esprits forts*; a 17th-century term for intellectuals or free-thinkers who sought to use reason over dogma</note>. When a **weak mind** sees that one is trying to prove the truths of the **Faith**, he takes it at face value and suspects nothing; but a **strong mind** easily untangles in a book what has been put there for the weak or for himself, and he easily distinguishes what is essential from what is **expedient** <note>original: *politique*; in this context, it refers to strategic or diplomatic choices made to avoid trouble with the authorities</note>. 

It was part of his prudence to first dazzle the **monks** and their partisans with a **sophism** <term>sophism: a clever but potentially misleading argument used here to satisfy religious critics</term> on the **existence of God**, and by a plausible supposition that He is the sole **mover of matter**. By this means, one shields oneself from the persecution of those false Christians, who cannot endure that **Philosophy** should be used to preserve the obscurity of **Faith**, and who want…

Jean le Brun is now presenting a radical philosophical argument: if matter is defined simply as "extension" (taking up space), and we cannot imagine "nothingness" or the absence of space, then matter must be eternal rather than created by God.</meta>

# NOUVEAUX

<page-num>74</page-num>

...added he, "that extension—that is to say, length, width, and depth—is the essence of matter <note>Le Brun is referencing the Cartesian view held by René Descartes, who argued that the fundamental nature of physical substance is *extensio* (extension), or simply occupying space.</note>. Even if that were so," I replied, "would it follow that God did not create it?"

"Yes," he retorted, "because it would follow that it is impossible to imagine a moment when this matter does not exist; and here is the little argument I make, to which there is certainly no answer. One must necessarily say that a thing exists when one cannot in any way conceive of it not existing. Now, the fact is that one cannot in any way conceive that matter does not exist."

"Why not?" I interrupted.

"It is impossible," he continued, "that before the world was created, this space which the world occupies did not exist. One cannot fail to conceive of this space. Now, it is impossible to conceive of this space without conceiving..."

<summary>Jean le Brun uses Cartesian logic to argue that matter (defined as extension) is eternal. He claims that because human reason cannot conceive of a true void or a "time before space," matter must have always existed, thereby challenging the doctrine of creation.</summary>
<keywords>extension, essence of matter, Descartes, existence, space, creation, philosophical reasoning</keywords>

"Make then an act of Faith, my son," he resumed, "concerning the creation of matter, and begin in the name of God to make your Faith triumph over Praxeas, over Hermogenes, and over the Platonists <note>Praxeas and Hermogenes were early Christian figures whose views were often declared heretical; Hermogenes specifically argued that matter was eternal. For who does not see that since matter is essentially long, wide, and deep, its parts must be so as well? And since an equal length, width, and depth form a die or a **cubic body**, it is impossible that this cubic body would not have some weight and tend downward; and thus, as all these cubic bodies encounter one another, they move each other in many directions. Finally, from the different assembly resulting from their movement, there emerge bodies of various shapes and diverse **machines** <note>In the 17th century, "machines" referred to any complex physical body, including living organisms, which were thought to function according to mechanical laws.</note>.

<meta>The dialogue continues with the radical philosopher (Jean le Brun) expanding on his argument against the possibility of the Incarnation. Having established that spirit and matter are fundamentally different substances, he now explains how they appear to interact through the lens of Occasionalism—the belief that God is the only true cause of all events.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>CONVERSATIONS. 105</header>

---

Following what we have said, the soul is not united to the body in such a way that it could be the cause of the actions and movements of the body. Assuming that God is the sole Author of these things, all that can be said to explain the union of the soul to the body is that God has established a certain relationship between the body and the soul, and that He has made a <term>pact<note>This refers to the "laws of the union of soul and body." In the philosophy of Occasionalism, God does not intervene randomly, but according to constant laws He has established, acting as a "pact" between the physical and spiritual realms.</note></term> that every time such a movement occurs in the body, He would produce such a thought in the soul; and that every time the soul thinks in such a manner, such a movement would be produced in the body. Thus, when God agitates the air, after having ignited the powder in a pistol, and when, on the occasion of this agitated air, He moves certain small nerves that correspond to the <term>pineal gland<note>A small organ in the brain which many 17th-century thinkers, following René Descartes, believed was the "seat of the soul" where physical sensations were transformed into mental perceptions.</note></term>, He executes the pact He made to produce in our soul that thought which is called...

<summary>The philosopher explains the theory of Occasionalism, arguing that because the soul and body are distinct, they do not interact directly; instead, God acts as the constant intermediary who synchronizes mental thoughts with physical actions according to established laws.</summary>

### FIFTH CONVERSATION

"Good evening, Mr. Jean le Brun," I said, returning to my room after having written, "have you supped well and without distraction?" "Very well, by the grace of God," he answered me, "I have meditated during the entire meal on the extravagance of certain Heretics, whom I saw in Germany, called <term>Ubiquitarians: from the Latin 'ubique' (everywhere); a name given to certain Lutheran theologians who argued that Christ's human nature, and thus his physical body, is present everywhere at once</term>, who believe they receive communion every time they eat, because they imagine that the Body of Jesus Christ is everywhere. Must one not be insane to say that? For if extension <note>In 17th-century philosophy, 'extension' refers to the property of taking up physical space</note> and impenetrability <note>The principle that two bodies cannot occupy the same space at the same time</note> are of the essence of matter, is it not as impossible that one body be received into another body of equal or smaller extension, as it is impossible that a cubic body of nine feet be

### DISCOURSES.

[...distin]guished Saint Thomas for these three effects of quantity. He said that this Saint, whom he praised infinitely, ratified the philosophy of Aristotle and accommodated it to the Faith, though through an <note>original: "modestie Angelique"; a reference to Thomas Aquinas’s traditional title, the "Angelic Doctor"</note> angelic modesty he often conceals the failings of this Philosopher, so as to avoid the praise he deserves for having corrected him; and that he is content to modestly explain his obscurities and errors, giving them a turn and a meaning conforming to the truths of the Faith, in which he deserves, without doubt, more praise than all the founders of <term>sects: here referring to competing philosophical schools or factions</term> and all the inventors of new opinions. This <term>Master of Arts: a university degree-holder licensed to teach the liberal arts, including logic and philosophy</term> won my heart in favor of Saint Thomas; that is why, Sir, if you do not wish to fall out with me, I beg you not to dismiss as mere imagination the thoughts of the most solid and most wise—

You know well, for example, that it is the Faith of the Church that the accidents <term>Accidents: In traditional philosophy, "accidents" are the qualities of a thing—like its color, smell, or taste—that can be perceived by the senses but are not part of its essential nature (the substance).</term> of the bread and the wine remain after the consecration; this is the language of the Fathers, the Popes, and the Councils. The Council of Constance, Pope Martin III, and the Roman Council under John XXII, the Council of Trent, and that of Cologne are all explicit on this point. However, our Philosophy <note>Refers to Cartesianism, the "new" mechanical philosophy of the 17th century.</note> demonstrates that there are no "accidents" in nature, that everything is substance because everything is matter, and that the different arrangement of the parts of matter creates all the machines, all the colors, all the sounds, and everything that we feel and see. “You see, Monsieur,” I replied, “I do not doubt that all our colleagues, the Reformers of Morality <note>This likely refers to the Jansenists or other rigorist groups in the 17th century who sought to reform Church practice and return to the teachings of the early Church Fathers.</note>, are searching with great care and providing passages from the Fathers to combat the philosophy of accidents; but I see very great drawbacks to this. “It would be ridiculous to say,” he retorted, “that the Fathers knew this philosophy; no one would believe it.”

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>CONVERSATIONS. 157</header>

## SIXTH CONVERSATION.

Scarcely was it daybreak when the venerable Jean le Brun knocked loudly at my door. The servants cursed him; and after having finally opened for him, they came to tell me at my bedside that the pilgrim—that great drinker—was asking to speak to me on a matter of importance. "Let him enter," I said, "and let us be left alone." "Mr. Jean le Brun," I said to him, upon seeing him enter, "did some unfortunate adventure befall you last night, and do you come so early to seek my assistance?" "Far from it," he replied; "I hurried to come, even before having performed my <term>Meditation</term> <note>A formal spiritual exercise or mental prayer, often practiced at the start of the day in 17th-century religious life.</note>, to tell you news that will rejoice you." "And what is it?" I asked. "It is that you are <term>Predestined</term> <note>Referring to the doctrine of Predestination—the belief that God has already chosen those who will receive eternal salvation. This was a highly contentious topic between Catholics, Jansenists, and Protestants.</note>: Moses told me so." The news—

<sig>Volume II. O</sig>

<summary>Jean le Brun arrives at the speaker's home early in the morning, bypassing his usual devotions to deliver a startling revelation: he claims that Moses appeared to him and confirmed the speaker is among the "Predestined."</summary>
<keywords>Jean le Brun, Predestination, Moses, Meditation, morning visit, theological revelation</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the narrator and the religious enthusiast Jean le Brun from page 157. Le Brun has just arrived at the narrator's bedside to claim that the narrator is "Predestined" according to a vision of Moses.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>NEW.</header>
<meta>catchword: gnon</meta>

"The news is joyful," I replied, "and all the more so because you have it from such a good source. But still, what dealings do you have with Moses?"

"I had never seen him until this night," he replied; "I went to bed yesterday evening in great anxiety over the difficulty you raised for me <note>Referring to the narrator's theological or philosophical objections discussed in the previous conversation.</note>. I fell asleep nonetheless; and at dawn, at the hour when God is accustomed to sending Heavenly Visions, Moses appeared to me. After having thanked me on God's behalf for the long labors I have endured for the reformation of Morality <note>A common 17th-century theme involving the movement to tighten religious discipline and ethical conduct against perceived "laxism."</note>, he told me that God has predestined you to be the Staff of my Old Age, the <term>Coadjutor</term> <note>An assistant or designated successor, often used for a high-ranking cleric who assists a bishop.</note> of my Designs, and the Heir of my Zeal. "But are all these five things really true?" I asked him.
"They must certainly be," he replied; "Monsieur Descartes founded this entire Philosophy upon them."
"There is then some appearance," I resumed, "that he was quite certain of it. Well, then, with that will you explain to us everything that beasts do?"

## FINAL CONVERSATION.

I thought I was rid of Jean le Brun; but the following day a young servant girl came to deliver a note from him, phrased in these terms.

> *This Creature of God <note>original: "Cette Créature de Dieu"—a humble, pious way of referring to a person of low social standing or a servant, common in 17th-century devout circles to emphasize that all are equal before the Creator.</note> will tell you, Monsieur, that I am very ill, and that a great affliction has befallen me which will soon bring me to the grave. It is important for the glory of God that I see you before I die.*

This note surprised me. I asked the Creature of God where her master was staying; and having learned from her that it was near the Petites-Maisons <term>Petites-Maisons</term> <note>Literally "Small Houses," this was a well-known Parisian hospital and hospice located in the Faubourg Saint-Germain, primarily used for the elderly, the infirm, and those deemed "insane."</note>, I promised her I would go there in an hour, and I did indeed go. I found the Creature of God giving some broth to Jean le Brun. "Come, my son," he cried out, "come and console a man who esteems you enough to par-"

"With all due respect, Monsieur," I replied, "you will find yourself quite as embarrassed as he; for you have taken the trouble—you, your great-great-grandfather <note>Original: *Trifaÿeul*. He is the one who proposes it, examines it, and who finally seems to have decided the matter just as you have, using the same reasons you allege; it is no great... <note>The sentence ends mid-thought; based on the catchword "mer-", the full phrase is likely "no great wonder" (*pas grande merveille*).</note>

was of his opinion on the way in which sensation occurs: That he was delighted that this Philosopher had, for once in his life, known the truth, and that he had realized that all sensations are made through touch. <note>In mechanistic philosophy, all senses (sight, sound, etc.) were explained as physical contact between particles, essentially reducing everything to the sense of touch.</note> Since I saw that this was the only good part in Aristotle, I resolved not to waste any time reading him.

He appeared moved by my rebuke, and I believe that, had he lived, he would not have been quite so foolish; but since the illness of *passion of the soul* <note>original: "paſſion d'animo"; an Italianate-Latin expression suggesting a fatal psychological or emotional distress or a "broken heart."</note> is always fatal, when I wished to return to see him the next day, I found the Creature of God all in tears, who told me that she had closed his eyes. I am quite saddened by it, for apparently he is damned.
